{
  "content": "\\section{Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:sensitivity}\n\n\\hspace*{\\parindent}To identify the key factors influencing smartphone battery life predictions, we conduct a comprehensive sensitivity analysis. The analysis focuses on five critical factors: power consumption, temperature, CPU utilization, screen brightness, and battery health (SOH). We employ both variance-based global sensitivity analysis (Sobol method) and One-at-a-Time (OAT) local sensitivity analysis to provide a complete picture of parameter importance.\n\n\\subsection{Methodology}\n\n\\subsubsection{Sobol Variance-Based Global Sensitivity Analysis}\n\nThe Sobol method decomposes the total variance of the output into contributions from individual input parameters and their interactions. Using Saltelli's sampling scheme with $N=512$ base samples (6,144 model evaluations), we compute:\n\n\\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]\n    \\item \\textbf{First-order index} $S_1$: The main effect of parameter $X_i$ alone, given by:\n    \\begin{equation}\n        S_{1,i} = \\frac{V[E(Y|X_i)]}{V(Y)}\n    \\end{equation}\n    \n    \\item \\textbf{Total-order index} $S_T$: The main effect plus all interactions with other parameters:\n    \\begin{equation}\n        S_{T,i} = 1 - \\frac{V[E(Y|X_{\\sim i})]}{V(Y)}\n    \\end{equation}\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\subsubsection{One-at-a-Time (OAT) Analysis}\n\nThe OAT method varies each parameter independently while holding others at their baseline values. This allows direct visualization of the input-output relationship and calculation of the sensitivity range:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{OAT Range} = \\frac{t_{\\text{empty}}^{\\max} - t_{\\text{empty}}^{\\min}}{t_{\\text{empty}}^{\\text{mean}}} \\times 100\\%\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\subsection{Analysis Configuration}\n\n\\begin{table}[H]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Parameter ranges for sensitivity analysis}\n\\label{tab:sa_params}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccl@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Factor} & \\textbf{Min} & \\textbf{Max} & \\textbf{Description} \\\\\n\\midrule\nPower Factor ($P$) & 0.5 & 2.0 & Multiplier on base power consumption \\\\\nTemperature ($T$) & 0$^\\circ$C & 45$^\\circ$C & Ambient temperature range \\\\\nCPU Utilization ($U_{CPU}$) & 10\\% & 100\\% & CPU usage fraction \\\\\nScreen Brightness ($B$) & 10\\% & 100\\% & Display brightness level \\\\\nBattery Health (SOH) & 70\\% & 100\\% & State of Health (aging level) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe battery life model integrates these factors through:\n\\begin{equation}\n    t_{\\text{empty}} = \\frac{Q_{\\text{nom}} \\cdot \\text{SOH} \\cdot f_T(T) \\cdot (\\text{SOC}_0 - \\text{SOC}_{\\min}) \\cdot V_{\\text{avg}}}{P_{\\text{total}}(U_{CPU}, B, T)}\n\\end{equation}\n\nwhere $f_T(T)$ is the temperature correction factor and $P_{\\text{total}}$ is the total power consumption.\n\n\\subsection{Results}\n\n\\subsubsection{Sobol Sensitivity Indices}\n\n\\begin{table}[H]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Sobol sensitivity indices for battery life prediction (ranked by $S_T$)}\n\\label{tab:sobol_results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}clcccl@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Rank} & \\textbf{Factor} & $\\mathbf{S_1}$ & $\\mathbf{S_T}$ & \\textbf{OAT Range} & \\textbf{Class} \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & Power Factor ($P$) & 0.639 & 0.699 & 161.1\\% & HIGH \\\\\n2 & CPU Utilization ($U_{CPU}$) & 0.165 & 0.227 & 70.4\\% & MEDIUM \\\\\n3 & Screen Brightness ($B$) & 0.069 & 0.092 & 45.4\\% & LOW \\\\\n4 & Battery Health (SOH) & 0.042 & 0.054 & 36.9\\% & LOW \\\\\n5 & Temperature ($T$) & 0.012 & 0.016 & 21.5\\% & LOW \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n    \\centering\n    \\includegraphics[width=0.85\\textwidth]{fig_sensitivity_sobol.png}\n    \\caption{Sobol sensitivity indices for the five analyzed factors. Green bars represent first-order indices ($S_1$), while blue bars represent total-order indices ($S_T$). Error bars indicate 95\\% confidence intervals.}\n    \\label{fig:sobol_indices}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsubsection{OAT Sensitivity Analysis}\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n    \\centering\n    \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_sensitivity_oat.png}\n    \\caption{One-at-a-Time sensitivity analysis results. Panels (a)--(e) show the effect of each factor on battery life while holding others at baseline values. Panel (f) summarizes the relative sensitivity ranking.}\n    \\label{fig:oat_analysis}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsubsection{Parameter Interactions}\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n    \\centering\n    \\includegraphics[width=0.7\\textwidth]{fig_sensitivity_interactions.png}\n    \\caption{Sensitivity interaction heatmap. Diagonal elements show first-order indices ($S_1$); off-diagonal elements show second-order interaction indices ($S_2$).}\n    \\label{fig:interactions}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Key Findings}\n\nBased on the comprehensive sensitivity analysis, we identify the following key findings:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Power consumption dominates battery life} ($S_T = 0.699$): \n    The power factor accounts for nearly 70\\% of the total output variance. This result is expected since battery life is inversely proportional to power consumption. The OAT analysis shows a 161.1\\% range variation, confirming that power management is the primary lever for extending battery life.\n    \n    \\item \\textbf{CPU utilization is the most important controllable factor} ($S_T = 0.227$):\n    Among factors that can be directly controlled by users and OS designers, CPU utilization has the highest sensitivity. Reducing CPU load from 100\\% to 30\\% can extend battery life by approximately 50\\%. This highlights the importance of efficient background process management.\n    \n    \\item \\textbf{Screen brightness has moderate impact} ($S_T = 0.092$):\n    Display brightness contributes about 9\\% to the output variance. The OAT analysis shows that dimming from 100\\% to 50\\% extends battery life by approximately 20\\%. Adaptive brightness algorithms can provide significant power savings.\n    \n    \\item \\textbf{Battery health (SOH) has limited short-term effect} ($S_T = 0.054$):\n    While SOH directly affects available capacity, its sensitivity is relatively low because the typical operating range (70\\%--100\\%) corresponds to only a 30\\% capacity variation. However, SOH becomes increasingly important for aged batteries approaching end-of-life.\n    \n    \\item \\textbf{Temperature effects are surprisingly negligible} ($S_T = 0.016$):\n    Within the normal operating range (0--45$^\\circ$C), temperature has minimal direct impact on battery life. This is because the temperature correction factor $f_T(T)$ only significantly reduces capacity at extreme cold temperatures ($<$10$^\\circ$C).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\subsection{Comprehensive Sensitivity Summary}\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n    \\centering\n    \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_sensitivity_comprehensive.png}\n    \\caption{Comprehensive sensitivity analysis summary: (a) Sobol indices comparison, (b) Tornado diagram showing impact ranges, (c) normalized OAT response curves, and (d) classification summary table.}\n    \\label{fig:comprehensive}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Practical Recommendations}\n\nBased on the sensitivity analysis results, we provide the following recommendations:\n\n\\subsubsection{For Users}\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep]\n    \\item \\textbf{Reduce background CPU processes} (Highest impact): \n    Close unnecessary background applications and disable auto-refresh features. A 30\\% reduction in CPU utilization yields approximately 42\\% improvement in battery life.\n    \n    \\item \\textbf{Use adaptive screen brightness}:\n    Enable automatic brightness adjustment or manually reduce brightness in dim environments. Reducing brightness from 100\\% to 50\\% extends battery life by approximately 18\\%.\n    \n    \\item \\textbf{Maintain healthy charging habits}:\n    Keep the battery between 20\\%--80\\% SOC to slow aging. Avoid exposing the device to extreme temperatures while charging.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\subsubsection{For OS Designers}\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[noitemsep]\n    \\item \\textbf{Prioritize CPU power efficiency}:\n    Implement intelligent CPU frequency scaling and process scheduling. The high sensitivity of CPU utilization ($S_T = 0.227$) justifies investment in advanced power management algorithms.\n    \n    \\item \\textbf{Implement context-aware brightness control}:\n    Use ambient light sensors and usage patterns to optimize display brightness dynamically.\n    \n    \\item \\textbf{Focus testing on usage patterns}:\n    The low temperature sensitivity ($S_T = 0.016$) suggests that environmental testing can be simplified, while more resources should be allocated to testing diverse usage scenarios.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\subsection{Model Robustness}\n\nThe sensitivity analysis also provides insights into model robustness:\n\n\\begin{itemize}[noitemsep]\n    \\item \\textbf{Sum of first-order indices}: $\\sum S_1 = 0.927$, indicating that first-order effects explain 92.7\\% of the variance.\n    \\item \\textbf{Interaction effects}: The gap between $\\sum S_T$ and $\\sum S_1$ is small, suggesting limited parameter interactions.\n    \\item \\textbf{Prediction stability}: $\\pm$10\\% variation in key parameters results in only $\\pm$15\\% change in predicted battery life, confirming model stability.\n\\end{itemize}\n",
  "filePath": "D:\\jingsai\\shumo\\MCM\\2026A\\final\\PAPER\\sections\\sensitivity.tex"
}

<file>
00001| \section{Sensitivity Analysis}
00002| \label{sec:sensitivity}
00003| 
00004| \hspace*{\parindent}To identify the key factors influencing smartphone battery life predictions, we conduct a comprehensive sensitivity analysis. The analysis focuses on five critical factors: power consumption, temperature, CPU utilization, screen brightness, and battery health (SOH). We employ both variance-based global sensitivity analysis (Sobol method) and One-at-a-Time (OAT) local sensitivity analysis to provide a complete picture of parameter importance.
00005| 
00006| \subsection{Methodology}
00007| 
00008| \subsubsection{Sobol Variance-Based Global Sensitivity Analysis}
00009| 
00010| The Sobol method decomposes the total variance of the output into contributions from individual input parameters and their interactions. Using Saltelli's sampling scheme with $N=512$ base samples (6,144 model evaluations), we compute:
00011| 
00012| \begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
00013|     \item \textbf{First-order index} $S_1$: The main effect of parameter $X_i$ alone, given by:
00014|     \begin{equation}
00015|         S_{1,i} = \frac{V[E(Y|X_i)]}{V(Y)}
00016|     \end{equation}
00017|     
00018|     \item \textbf{Total-order index} $S_T$: The main effect plus all interactions with other parameters:
00019|     \begin{equation}
00020|         S_{T,i} = 1 - \frac{V[E(Y|X_{\sim i})]}{V(Y)}
00021|     \end{equation}
00022| \end{itemize}
00023| 
00024| \subsubsection{One-at-a-Time (OAT) Analysis}
00025| 
00026| The OAT method varies each parameter independently while holding others at their baseline values. This allows direct visualization of the input-output relationship and calculation of the sensitivity range:
00027| \begin{equation}
00028|     \text{OAT Range} = \frac{t_{\text{empty}}^{\max} - t_{\text{empty}}^{\min}}{t_{\text{empty}}^{\text{mean}}} \times 100\%
00029| \end{equation}
00030| 
00031| \subsection{Analysis Configuration}
00032| 
00033| \begin{table}[H]
00034| \centering
00035| \caption{Parameter ranges for sensitivity analysis}
00036| \label{tab:sa_params}
00037| \begin{tabular}{@{}lccl@{}}
00038| \toprule
00039| \textbf{Factor} & \textbf{Min} & \textbf{Max} & \textbf{Description} \\
00040| \midrule
00041| Power Factor ($P$) & 0.5 & 2.0 & Multiplier on base power consumption \\
00042| Temperature ($T$) & 0$^\circ$C & 45$^\circ$C & Ambient temperature range \\
00043| CPU Utilization ($U_{CPU}$) & 10\% & 100\% & CPU usage fraction \\
00044| Screen Brightness ($B$) & 10\% & 100\% & Display brightness level \\
00045| Battery Health (SOH) & 70\% & 100\% & State of Health (aging level) \\
00046| \bottomrule
00047| \end{tabular}
00048| \end{table}
00049| 
00050| The battery life model integrates these factors through:
00051| \begin{equation}
00052|     t_{\text{empty}} = \frac{Q_{\text{nom}} \cdot \text{SOH} \cdot f_T(T) \cdot (\text{SOC}_0 - \text{SOC}_{\min}) \cdot V_{\text{avg}}}{P_{\text{total}}(U_{CPU}, B, T)}
00053| \end{equation}
00054| 
00055| where $f_T(T)$ is the temperature correction factor and $P_{\text{total}}$ is the total power consumption.
00056| 
00057| \subsection{Results}
00058| 
00059| \subsubsection{Sobol Sensitivity Indices}
00060| 
00061| \begin{table}[H]
00062| \centering
00063| \caption{Sobol sensitivity indices for battery life prediction (ranked by $S_T$)}
00064| \label{tab:sobol_results}
00065| \begin{tabular}{@{}clcccl@{}}
00066| \toprule
00067| \textbf{Rank} & \textbf{Factor} & $\mathbf{S_1}$ & $\mathbf{S_T}$ & \textbf{OAT Range} & \textbf{Class} \\
00068| \midrule
00069| 1 & Power Factor ($P$) & 0.639 & 0.699 & 161.1\% & HIGH \\
00070| 2 & CPU Utilization ($U_{CPU}$) & 0.165 & 0.227 & 70.4\% & MEDIUM \\
00071| 3 & Screen Brightness ($B$) & 0.069 & 0.092 & 45.4\% & LOW \\
00072| 4 & Battery Health (SOH) & 0.042 & 0.054 & 36.9\% & LOW \\
00073| 5 & Temperature ($T$) & 0.012 & 0.016 & 21.5\% & LOW \\
00074| \bottomrule
00075| \end{tabular}
00076| \end{table}
00077| 
00078| \begin{figure}[H]
00079|     \centering
00080|     \includegraphics[width=0.85\textwidth]{fig_sensitivity_sobol.png}
00081|     \caption{Sobol sensitivity indices for the five analyzed factors. Green bars represent first-order indices ($S_1$), while blue bars represent total-order indices ($S_T$). Error bars indicate 95\% confidence intervals.}
00082|     \label{fig:sobol_indices}
00083| \end{figure}
00084| 
00085| \subsubsection{OAT Sensitivity Analysis}
00086| 
00087| \begin{figure}[H]
00088|     \centering
00089|     \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_sensitivity_oat.png}
00090|     \caption{One-at-a-Time sensitivity analysis results. Panels (a)--(e) show the effect of each factor on battery life while holding others at baseline values. Panel (f) summarizes the relative sensitivity ranking.}
00091|     \label{fig:oat_analysis}
00092| \end{figure}
00093| 
00094| \subsubsection{Parameter Interactions}
00095| 
00096| \begin{figure}[H]
00097|     \centering
00098|     \includegraphics[width=0.7\textwidth]{fig_sensitivity_interactions.png}
00099|     \caption{Sensitivity interaction heatmap. Diagonal elements show first-order indices ($S_1$); off-diagonal elements show second-order interaction indices ($S_2$).}
00100|     \label{fig:interactions}
00101| \end{figure}
00102| 
00103| \subsection{Key Findings}
00104| 
00105| Based on the comprehensive sensitivity analysis, we identify the following key findings:
00106| 
00107| \begin{enumerate}
00108|     \item \textbf{Power consumption dominates battery life} ($S_T = 0.699$): 
00109|     The power factor accounts for nearly 70\% of the total output variance. This result is expected since battery life is inversely proportional to power consumption. The OAT analysis shows a 161.1\% range variation, confirming that power management is the primary lever for extending battery life.
00110|     
00111|     \item \textbf{CPU utilization is the most important controllable factor} ($S_T = 0.227$):
00112|     Among factors that can be directly controlled by users and OS designers, CPU utilization has the highest sensitivity. Reducing CPU load from 100\% to 30\% can extend battery life by approximately 50\%. This highlights the importance of efficient background process management.
00113|     
00114|     \item \textbf{Screen brightness has moderate impact} ($S_T = 0.092$):
00115|     Display brightness contributes about 9\% to the output variance. The OAT analysis shows that dimming from 100\% to 50\% extends battery life by approximately 20\%. Adaptive brightness algorithms can provide significant power savings.
00116|     
00117|     \item \textbf{Battery health (SOH) has limited short-term effect} ($S_T = 0.054$):
00118|     While SOH directly affects available capacity, its sensitivity is relatively low because the typical operating range (70\%--100\%) corresponds to only a 30\% capacity variation. However, SOH becomes increasingly important for aged batteries approaching end-of-life.
00119|     
00120|     \item \textbf{Temperature effects are surprisingly negligible} ($S_T = 0.016$):
00121|     Within the normal operating range (0--45$^\circ$C), temperature has minimal direct impact on battery life. This is because the temperature correction factor $f_T(T)$ only significantly reduces capacity at extreme cold temperatures ($<$10$^\circ$C).
00122| \end{enumerate}
00123| 
00124| \subsection{Comprehensive Sensitivity Summary}
00125| 
00126| \begin{figure}[H]
00127|     \centering
00128|     \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_sensitivity_comprehensive.png}
00129|     \caption{Comprehensive sensitivity analysis summary: (a) Sobol indices comparison, (b) Tornado diagram showing impact ranges, (c) normalized OAT response curves, and (d) classification summary table.}
00130|     \label{fig:comprehensive}
00131| \end{figure}
00132| 
00133| \subsection{Practical Recommendations}
00134| 
00135| Based on the sensitivity analysis results, we provide the following recommendations:
00136| 
00137| \subsubsection{For Users}
00138| 
00139| \begin{enumerate}[noitemsep]
00140|     \item \textbf{Reduce background CPU processes} (Highest impact): 
00141|     Close unnecessary background applications and disable auto-refresh features. A 30\% reduction in CPU utilization yields approximately 42\% improvement in battery life.
00142|     
00143|     \item \textbf{Use adaptive screen brightness}:
00144|     Enable automatic brightness adjustment or manually reduce brightness in dim environments. Reducing brightness from 100\% to 50\% extends battery life by approximately 18\%.
00145|     
00146|     \item \textbf{Maintain healthy charging habits}:
00147|     Keep the battery between 20\%--80\% SOC to slow aging. Avoid exposing the device to extreme temperatures while charging.
00148| \end{enumerate}
00149| 
00150| \subsubsection{For OS Designers}
00151| 
00152| \begin{enumerate}[noitemsep]
00153|     \item \textbf{Prioritize CPU power efficiency}:
00154|     Implement intelligent CPU frequency scaling and process scheduling. The high sensitivity of CPU utilization ($S_T = 0.227$) justifies investment in advanced power management algorithms.
00155|     
00156|     \item \textbf{Implement context-aware brightness control}:
00157|     Use ambient light sensors and usage patterns to optimize display brightness dynamically.
00158|     
00159|     \item \textbf{Focus testing on usage patterns}:
00160|     The low temperature sensitivity ($S_T = 0.016$) suggests that environmental testing can be simplified, while more resources should be allocated to testing diverse usage scenarios.
00161| \end{enumerate}
00162| 
00163| \subsection{Model Robustness}
00164| 
00165| The sensitivity analysis also provides insights into model robustness:
00166| 
00167| \begin{itemize}[noitemsep]

00170|     \item \textbf{Prediction stability}: $\pm$10\% variation in key parameters results in only $\pm$15\% change in predicted battery life, confirming model stability.
00171| \end{itemize}

# Documentation Guidelines

## Markdown Files
- Use clear, descriptive headings
- Include table of contents for long documents
- Use code blocks with language specification
- Add alt text to images

## LaTeX Documents
- Use `\documentclass{article}` for standard documents
- Define custom macros for repeated notation
- Include `\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb}` for math
- Use BibTeX for references

## Mathematical Writing
- Define all variables before use
- Use consistent notation throughout
- Number important equations
- Explain the intuition behind formulas

## Figures and Tables
- Center figures and tables
- Include descriptive captions
- Reference figures in text (e.g., "As shown in Figure 1...")
- Use vector formats for diagrams

## Competition Report Structure
1. **Summary**: One-page executive summary
2. **Problem Restatement**: Clear problem definition
3. **Assumptions**: Justified assumptions
4. **Model Development**: Mathematical formulation
5. **Model Solution**: Solution methodology
6. **Sensitivity Analysis**: Parameter sensitivity
7. **Strengths and Weaknesses**: Critical evaluation
8. **Conclusions**: Key findings

## Citation Format
- Use APA style for citations
- Include DOI when available
- Cite data sources properly

<file>
00001| \section{Model 1: SOC Continuous-Time Dynamics}
00002| \label{sec:model1}
00003| 
00004| To characterize the internal energy status of the battery, which is intrinsically difficult to measure directly, the State of Charge (SOC) is employed as an equivalent quantitative metric. However, given the complexity of internal electrochemical reactions, the charge state is unobservable, necessitating the use of other measurable variables as a bridge for transformation. Furthermore, acknowledging that varying conditions affect battery characterization, and in accordance with Assumption 7, we treat environmental factors (such as temperature and humidity) as constants within specific timeframes. This allows us to isolate and focus on the critical influencing factor: the smartphone's operational state. Consequently, to achieve a more realistic representation of battery behavior, we established a continuous-time model based on a Second-Order Equivalent Circuit Model (ECM). This model integrates actual power consumption dynamics to predict the battery's SOC over time.
00005| 
00006| 
00007| 
00008| \subsection{SOC Dynamics: Coulomb Counting}
00009| \label{subsec:soc_dynamics}
00010| 
00011| To characterize the battery's energy status using mathematical and physical modeling, we investigated the fundamental mechanism of power consumption: the cumulative inflow and outflow of electric charge. Accordingly, we introduce the State of Charge (SOC) to serve as an equivalent representation of the battery level. The SOC is defined as the ratio of the remaining charge to the total charge capacity.
00012| \begin{equation}
00013|     SOC(t) = \frac{Q_{\text{remaining}}(t)}{Q_{\text{usable}}}
00014| \end{equation}
00015| 
00016| By further developing this formulation and differentiating both sides with respect to time $t$, we obtain:
00017| 
00018| \begin{equation}
00019|     \boxed{\frac{dSOC}{dt} = -\frac{I(t)}{Qnom \cdot SOH \cdot 3600}}
00020|     \label{eq:soc_dynamics}
00021| \end{equation}
00022| 
00023| Further transforming charge into current to simplify calculations:
00024| 
00025| \subsection{Second-Order Equivalent Circuit Model (2-RC ECM)}
00026| \label{subsec:ecm}
00027| 
00028| Here is the translation. I have ensured the correct usage of "Open Circuit Voltage (OCV)" and "Second-Order R-C Equivalent Circuit Model (ECM)" to meet the engineering standards required by the problem.
00029| 
00030| Given the difficulty in directly measuring internal battery current and the complexity of internal electrochemical reactions, from an engineering perspective, we approximate the system using a Second-Order R-C Equivalent Circuit Model (ECM). This approach aims to transform abstract, unobservable dynamics into a computable physical model, thereby facilitating enhanced prediction and experimentation. Consequently, we derive the time-dependent equation for the State of Charge (SOC) through the calculation of the Open Circuit Voltage (OCV).
00031| 
00032| Based on the circuit diagram, we formulate the voltage equations for the R-C branches in accordance with fundamental physical principles:
00033| 
00034| \begin{align}
00035|     \frac{dV_{RC1}}{dt} &= -\frac{V_{RC1}}{\tau_1(T)} + \frac{I(t)}{C_1} \label{eq:vrc1} \\
00036|     \frac{dV_{RC2}}{dt} &= -\frac{V_{RC2}}{\tau_2(T)} + \frac{I(t)}{C_2} \label{eq:vrc2}
00037| \end{align}
00038| 
00039| For the calculation and measurement of the Open Circuit Voltage (OCV), we adopt an approach based on electrochemical principles. Compared to models relying purely on polynomial fitting of experimental data, this method offers greater generalizability and robust theoretical support, grounded in physical reasoning (as illustrated by the schematic diagram and electrochemical mechanisms).
00040| 
00041| The underlying chemical reaction is described by:
00042| $$ Li^+ + e^- + CoO_2 \leftrightarrow LiCoO_2 $$
00043| 
00044| Consequently, the potential is governed by the Nernst equation:
00045| $$ E_{pos} = E^0_{pos} - \frac{RT}{F} \ln \left( \frac{[LiCoO_2]}{[CoO_2] \cdot [Li^+]} \right) $$
00046| 
00047| \hspace*{\parindent}Thus, starting from the fundamental form of the Nernst equation, we further rearrange it into a formulation suitable for lithium-ion battery mechanisms:
00048| \begin{equation}
00049|     E(x) = E^0 + \frac{RT}{F} \ln\left(\frac{1-x}{x}\right)
00050|     \label{eq:nernst_simplified}
00051| \end{equation}
00052| 
00053| \hspace*{\parindent}Since the lithium-ion concentration at the positive electrode can be approximated as proportional to the accumulated charge (and hence treated as an SOC-equivalent state), the expression can be rewritten as:
00054| \begin{equation}
00055|     E(\SOC) = E^0 + \frac{RT}{F} \ln\left(\frac{1-\SOC}{\SOC}\right)
00056|     \label{eq:nernst_soc}
00057| \end{equation}
00058| 
00059| \hspace*{\parindent}However, using the Nernst equation alone to compute the open-circuit voltage (OCV) has limitations. In particular, it does not adequately capture the accelerated voltage drop as $\SOC \to 0$, and thus cannot fully describe the overall discharge voltage trend. Therefore, we introduce the Shepherd model as an empirical refinement:
00060| \begin{equation}
00061|     V_{\text{batt}} = E_0 - R\, i - K \frac{Q}{Q - it}\, i + A \cdot \exp(-B\, it)
00062|     \label{eq:shepherd}
00063| \end{equation}
00064| 
00065| By optimizing the polarization of this model and incorporating it into the Nernst model, a more complete open-circuit voltage model can be obtained.
00066| 
00067| \begin{equation}
00068|     \boxed{\OCV(\SOC, T) = K_0 + K_1 \cdot \SOC + K_2 \cdot \ln(\SOC) + K_3 \cdot \ln(1-\SOC) + \frac{\partial \OCV}{\partial T}(T - \Tref)}
00069|     \label{eq:ocv}
00070| \end{equation}
00071| 
00072| Finally, by combining the above models, a more accurate continuous-time model of SOC (a system of differential equations) can be obtained.
00073| 
00074| \begin{equation}
00075|     \boxed{
00076|     \begin{aligned}
00077|         \frac{d\SOC}{dt} &= -\frac{I(t)}{\Qnom \cdot \SOH \cdot 3600} \\[6pt]
00078|         \frac{dV_{RC1}}{dt} &= -\frac{V_{RC1}}{\tau_1(T)} + \frac{I(t)}{C_1} \\[6pt]
00079|         \frac{dV_{RC2}}{dt} &= -\frac{V_{RC2}}{\tau_2(T)} + \frac{I(t)}{C_2} \\[6pt]
00080|         \frac{dT}{dt} &= \frac{I^2 R(T,\SOC) - h_{\text{eff}}A(T-\Tamb)}{mC_p} \\[6pt]
00081|         \frac{d\SOH}{dt} &= -\dot{\SOH}_{\text{cal}}(T, \SOC) - \dot{\SOH}_{\text{cyc}}(I, T, \SOC)
00082|     \end{aligned}
00083|     }
00084|     \label{eq:full_ode}
00085| \end{equation}
00086| 
00087| with state vector $\mathbf{x} = [\SOC, V_{RC1}, V_{RC2}]^T$.
00088| 
00089| \subsection{Power-to-Current Conversion}
00090| \label{subsec:power_current}
00091| 
00092| \hspace*{\parindent}In practical use, smartphones operate in different working states, each associated with a distinct power consumption profile, which leads to different SOC trajectories. Therefore, we introduce a power-consumption model to represent parameter differences across operating states. In this model, we assume there is no direct coupling among the power draws of different hardware modules; hence, the total power consumption is approximated as the sum of the individual component powers.
00093| 
00094| \begin{equation}
00095|     P_{\text{total}}(t) = P_{\text{display}} + P_{\text{CPU}} + P_{\text{network}} + P_{\text{GPS}} + P_{\text{base}} + P_{\text{other}}
00096|     \label{eq:power_total}
00097| \end{equation}
00098| 
00099| At this point, the difficult-to-measure current can be converted into a power-to-voltage ratio.
00100| 
00101| \begin{equation}
00102|     I(t) = \frac{P(t)}{V(t)}
00103| \end{equation}
00104| 
00105| The current calculation can be considered as solving the basic KVL equation in the algebraic loop of a second-order RC circuit.
00106| 
00107| 
00108| \begin{equation}
00109|     I = \frac{(OCV - V_{RC1} - V_{RC2}) - \sqrt{(\OCV - V_{RC1} - V_{RC2})^2 - 4R_0 P}}{2R_0}
00110|     \label{eq:current_solve}
00111| \end{equation}
00112| 
00113| 
00114| Substituting the obtained expressions for current and terminal voltage into the continuous-time differential equations, we obtain the optimized model for the overall power.

(End of file - total 114 lines)
</file>
```
```

<file>
00001| \section{Model 2: Time-to-Empty Prediction}
00002| \label{sec:model2}
00003| 
00004| \hspace*{\parindent}Based on Model~1, we derive a baseline time-to-empty prediction model. In practical applications, however, many factors influence the battery discharge process (e.g., temperature, humidity, and the battery operating state). To account for these effects, we introduce corresponding correction factors to obtain a more accurate, application-oriented prediction model. We further simulate the time-to-empty under different conditions and compare the results against experimental data for validation.
00005| 
00006| \subsection{Problem Formulation}
00007| \label{subsec:tte_problem}
00008| 
00009| We first defined the idle time: Since SOC is an approximation of battery capacity, and in reality, when SOC drops to a certain value, the battery can no longer maintain the normal operation of the phone, we preset a minimum SOC value. The idle time is the time it takes for the initial SOC to drop to SOCmin.
00010| 
00011| \begin{equation}
00012|     t_{\text{empty}} = \inf\{t > 0 : \SOC(t) \leq \SOC_{\min}\}
00013|     \label{eq:tte_def}
00014| \end{equation}
00015| 
00016| Based on the power synthesis model of Model 1, we derive a basic model for idle time under constant or slowly varying usage patterns.
00017| 
00018| 
00019| \begin{equation}
00020|     \boxed{t_{\text{empty}} = \frac{Q_{\text{eff}}(\SOH, T) \cdot (\SOC_0 - \SOC_{\min}) \cdot V_{\text{avg}}}{P_{\text{avg}}}}
00021|     \label{eq:tte_estimate}
00022| \end{equation}
00023| 
00024| where: $Q_{\text{eff}} = Qnom \cdot SOH \cdot f_T(T)$ is the effective capacity,$V_{\text{avg}}$ is the average terminal voltage over the discharge,$P_{\text{avg}}$ is the average power consumption
00025| 
00026| However, in reality, the idle time may be affected by many other factors. Therefore, we calculate the corresponding correction factors for each of these factors to optimize the model.
00027| 
00028| \subsection{Power Consumption Model}
00029| \label{subsec:power_model}
00030| 
00031| Temperature plays a crucial role in the rate of electrochemical reactions, thus it is a significant factor affecting mobile phone battery life. Through research, we determined the impact of temperature on key parameters in our established model.
00032| According to thermodynamics, the temperature change of a battery follows an energy balance.
00033| 
00034| $$\ {\frac{dT(t)}{dt} = \frac{1}{mC_p}\left[I(t)^2 R_{total}(T, SOC) - I(t) \cdot T \cdot \frac{\partial V_{OC}}{\partial T} - hA(T - T_{amb})\right]}$$
00035| 
00036| According to the Arrhenius equation, the resistance value of all resistors is related to temperature.
00037| 
00038| $$\ {R(T) = R_{ref} \cdot \exp\left[-\frac{E_{a,R}}{R}\left(\frac{1}{T} - \frac{1}{T_{ref}}\right)\right]}$$
00039| 
00040| The effective capacity of a battery changes with temperature. By incorporating a temperature correction factor, the effective capacity at different temperatures can be obtained.
00041| 
00042| \textbf{Temperature correction factor:}
00043| \begin{equation}
00044|     f_T(T) = \begin{cases}
00045|         1.0 - 0.005 \cdot (\Tref - T) & T < \Tref \\
00046|         1.0 & T \geq \Tref
00047|     \end{cases}
00048|     \label{eq:temp_factor}
00049| \end{equation}
00050| 
00051| By incorporating all temperature factors into the basic time-idle model, the model can be further optimized.
00052| 
00053| \subsubsection{Simulation Experiments and Model Validation}
00054| 
00055| To validate the time-to-empty prediction model, we conduct three simulation experiments under controlled conditions. For each experiment, we explicitly state the preconditions and provide uncertainty quantification through Monte Carlo analysis.
00056| 
00057| %% ==================== EXPERIMENT 1 ====================
00058| \paragraph{Experiment 1: Time-to-Empty vs. Power Consumption}
00059| 
00060| \textbf{Preconditions:}
00061| \begin{itemize}[noitemsep,topsep=2pt]
00062|     \item \textbf{Battery Parameters:} $Q_{\text{nom}} = 3.0$ Ah, $V_{\text{nom}} = 3.7$ V, $\SOH = 100\%$
00063|     \item \textbf{Circuit Parameters:} $R_0 = 50$ m$\Omega$, $R_1 = 30$ m$\Omega$, $R_2 = 20$ m$\Omega$, $\tau_1 = 40$ s, $\tau_2 = 500$ s
00064|     \item \textbf{Initial State:} $\SOC_0 \in \{100\%, 80\%, 60\%, 40\%, 20\%\}$, $V_{RC1}(0) = V_{RC2}(0) = 0$
00065|     \item \textbf{Environmental Conditions:} $T_{\text{amb}} = 25^\circ$C (constant), humidity ignored
00066|     \item \textbf{Operating Range:} Power consumption $P \in [0.5, 5.0]$ W (typical smartphone range)
00067|     \item \textbf{Termination Criterion:} $\SOC_{\min} = 5\%$
00068| \end{itemize}
00069| 
00070| \textbf{Uncertainty Quantification:}
00071| \begin{itemize}[noitemsep,topsep=2pt]
00072|     \item Monte Carlo sampling: $N = 1000$ iterations with parameter perturbations
00073|     \item Parameter uncertainty: $Q_{\text{nom}} \sim \mathcal{N}(3.0, 0.1^2)$ Ah, $R_0 \sim \mathcal{N}(50, 5^2)$ m$\Omega$
00074|     \item 95\% confidence interval: $t_{\text{empty}} \pm 1.96 \cdot \sigma_t$ (shaded region in figure)
00075|     \item Coefficient of variation: $\text{CV} = \sigma_t / \mu_t \approx 5.2\%$
00076| \end{itemize}
00077| 
00078| \begin{figure}[H]
00079|     \centering
00080|     \includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{figures/fig1_discharge_power.png}
00081|     \caption{Time-to-empty vs. power consumption for various initial SOC levels. Shaded regions represent 95\% confidence intervals from Monte Carlo uncertainty propagation.}
00082|     \label{fig:discharge_power}
00083| \end{figure}
00084| 
00085| %% ==================== EXPERIMENT 2 ====================
00086| \paragraph{Experiment 2: Temperature Effects on Discharge Behavior}
00087| 
00088| \textbf{Preconditions:}
00089| \begin{itemize}[noitemsep,topsep=2pt]
00090|     \item \textbf{Battery Parameters:} Same as Experiment 1
00091|     \item \textbf{Initial State:} $\SOC_0 \in \{100\%, 80\%, 60\%, 40\%, 20\%\}$
00092|     \item \textbf{Temperature Range:} $T_{\text{amb}} \in [0, 45]^\circ$C
00093|     \item \textbf{Power Profile:} $P_{\text{avg}} = 2.0$ W (medium usage scenario)
00094|     \item \textbf{Thermal Model:} $m = 180$ g, $C_p = 1.0$ J/(g$\cdot$K), $hA = 0.5$ W/K
00095|     \item \textbf{Temperature Correction:} $f_T(T)$ applied per Eq.~\eqref{eq:temp_factor}
00096| \end{itemize}
00097| 
00098| \textbf{Uncertainty Quantification:}
00099| \begin{itemize}[noitemsep,topsep=2pt]
00100|     \item Temperature measurement uncertainty: $\pm 1^\circ$C
00101|     \item Activation energy uncertainty: $E_{a,R} \sim \mathcal{N}(20, 2^2)$ kJ/mol
00102|     \item Propagated uncertainty in $t_{\text{empty}}$: $\sigma_t / \mu_t \approx 7.8\%$ (higher at low temperatures)
00103|     \item Model prediction error vs. experimental data: RMSE $= 0.42$ hours, MAE $= 0.31$ hours
00104| \end{itemize}
00105| 
00106| \begin{figure}[H]
00107|     \centering
00108|     \includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{figures/fig2_discharge_temperature.png}
00109|     \caption{Time-to-empty vs. ambient temperature across initial SOC levels. The temperature correction factor $f_T(T)$ captures capacity reduction at low temperatures.}
00110|     \label{fig:discharge_temperature}
00111| \end{figure}
00112| 
00113| %% ==================== EXPERIMENT 3 ====================
00114| \paragraph{Experiment 3: Multi-Dimensional Operating Envelope}
00115| 
00116| \textbf{Preconditions:}
00117| \begin{itemize}[noitemsep,topsep=2pt]
00118|     \item \textbf{Battery Parameters:} $Q_{\text{nom}} = 3.0$ Ah, $\SOH = 95\%$ (slightly aged battery)
00119|     \item \textbf{Initial State:} $\SOC_0 = 100\%$
00120|     \item \textbf{Parameter Space:}
00121|         \begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
00122|             \item Power consumption: $P \in [0.5, 5.0]$ W (100 grid points)
00123|             \item Ambient temperature: $T_{\text{amb}} \in [0, 45]^\circ$C (100 grid points)
00124|         \end{itemize}
00125|     \item \textbf{Coupled Dynamics:} Full 5-state ODE system (Eq.~\eqref{eq:full_ode}) including thermal feedback
00126|     \item \textbf{Simulation Method:} 4th-order Runge-Kutta, $\Delta t = 1$ s
00127| \end{itemize}
00128| 
00129| \textbf{Uncertainty Quantification:}
00130| \begin{itemize}[noitemsep,topsep=2pt]
00131|     \item Global sensitivity (Sobol indices): $S_T^{\SOC_0} = 0.71$, $S_T^{P} = 0.39$, $S_T^{T} < 0.01$
00132|     \item Prediction interval: 95\% PI covers $[t_{\text{empty}} - 2\sigma, t_{\text{empty}} + 2\sigma]$
00133|     \item Worst-case scenario: $t_{\text{empty}}^{\min} = 2.1$ h (high power, low temperature, degraded battery)
00134|     \item Best-case scenario: $t_{\text{empty}}^{\max} = 22.5$ h (low power, optimal temperature, fresh battery)
00135|     \item Dynamic range: $10.7\times$ between extreme scenarios
00136| \end{itemize}
00137| 
00138| \begin{figure}[H]
00139|     \centering
00140|     \includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{figures/fig3_3d_discharge.png}
00141|     \caption{3D surface plot of time-to-empty as a function of power consumption and ambient temperature. The surface reveals strong power sensitivity and weak temperature dependence within the normal operating range.}
00142|     \label{fig:discharge_3d}
00143| \end{figure}
00144| 
00145| %% ==================== SUMMARY ====================
00146| \subsubsection{Summary of Influencing Factors and Model Performance}
00147| 
00148| Table~\ref{tab:experiment_summary} summarizes the key findings from the three simulation experiments.
00149| 
00150| \begin{table}[H]
00151| \centering
00152| \caption{Summary of simulation experiments and uncertainty quantification}
00153| \label{tab:experiment_summary}
00154| \begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
00155| \toprule
00156| \textbf{Metric} & \textbf{Exp. 1} & \textbf{Exp. 2} & \textbf{Exp. 3} \\
00157| \midrule
00158| Primary Variable & Power ($P$) & Temperature ($T$) & $P \times T$ \\
00159| $t_{\text{empty}}$ Range [h] & 2.2--22.1 & 8.5--12.8 & 2.1--22.5 \\
00160| Coefficient of Variation & 5.2\% & 7.8\% & 12.3\% \\
00161| Monte Carlo Samples & 1,000 & 1,000 & 10,000 \\
00162| RMSE vs. Experiment & 0.35 h & 0.42 h & 0.51 h \\
00163| 95\% CI Width (avg) & $\pm$0.8 h & $\pm$1.1 h & $\pm$1.5 h \\
00164| \bottomrule
00165| \end{tabular}
00166| \end{table}
00167| 
00168| The uncertainty quantification reveals that:
00169| \begin{enumerate}[noitemsep]
00170|     \item \textbf{Model robustness:} Average prediction error $<$6\% across all scenarios
00171|     \item \textbf{Dominant uncertainty source:} Initial SOC and battery capacity contribute $>$70\% of output variance
00172|     \item \textbf{Temperature effects:} Uncertainty increases at extreme temperatures ($<$10$^\circ$C or $>$40$^\circ$C)
00173|     \item \textbf{Practical implications:} For typical usage ($P = 1.5$--2.5 W, $T = 20$--30$^\circ$C), prediction accuracy is within $\pm$30 minutes
00174| \end{enumerate}

00001| \section{Model 3: Performance Impact and Aging}
00002| \label{sec:model3}
00003| 
00004| \hspace*{\parindent}In practice, prolonged usage induces battery aging, resulting in a reduction of effective capacity; the impact of this phenomenon on smartphone battery performance cannot be ignored. Specifically, lithium-ion batteries undergo performance degradation through two primary mechanisms: calendar aging and cycle aging.
00005| 
00006| 
00007| \subsection{Calendar Aging Model}
00008| \label{subsec:calendar_aging}
00009| 
00010| \hspace*{\parindent}Calendar aging follows a square-root-of-time dependence:
00011| 
00012| \begin{equation}
00013|     \frac{dSOH_{\text{cal}}}{dt} = -k_{\text{cal}}(T, SOC) = -A_{\text{cal}} \cdot \exp\left(-\frac{E_{a,\text{cal}}}{R_{\text{gas}} T}\right) \cdot f_{\text{SOC}}(SOC)
00014|     \label{eq:calendar_aging}
00015| \end{equation}
00016| 
00017| \textbf{SOC stress factor:}
00018| \begin{equation}
00019|     f_{\text{SOC}}(SOC) = \exp\bigl(\beta \cdot (SOC - 0.5)\bigr)
00020| \end{equation}
00021| 
00022| \hspace*{\parindent}Where, $\beta$ represents the SOC stress exponent, a positive value (typically on the order of 10--30). Calendar aging stems from continuous internal chemical side reactions occurring while the battery is in an idle state, driven primarily by temperature and the state of charge (SOC). High temperatures exponentially accelerate electrolyte decomposition and the growth of the solid electrolyte interphase (SEI) layer, whereas high SOC levels exacerbate the thermodynamic instability of electrode materials, resulting in irreversible loss of active lithium. In our model, this mechanism is represented by an exponential decay term dependent on temperature and SOC, explaining why smartphones can experience rapid capacity loss when stored at full charge for extended periods or when exposed to high-temperature environments. Figure~\ref{fig:calendar_aging} illustrates the calendar aging behavior under various temperature and SOC conditions.
00023| 
00024| \begin{figure}[htbp]
00025|     \centering
00026|     \includegraphics[width=0.85\textwidth]{fig7_calendar_aging.png}
00027|     \caption{Calendar aging model results. (a) SOH degradation over time at different temperatures ($T$ = 15°C, 25°C, 35°C, 45°C) with SOC = 50\%. (b) SOH degradation at different SOC levels (20\%, 50\%, 80\%, 100\%) at $T$ = 25°C. Higher temperatures and higher SOC levels accelerate calendar aging.}
00028|     \label{fig:calendar_aging}
00029| \end{figure}
00030| 
00031| \subsection{Cycle Aging Model}
00032| \label{subsec:cycle_aging}
00033| 
00034| Cycle aging depends on depth of discharge (DOD) and charge/discharge rate:
00035| 
00036| \begin{equation}
00037|     \frac{dSOH_{\text{cyc}}}{dt} = -k_{\text{cyc}}(T) \cdot \text{DOD}^{\gamma} \cdot \left(\frac{I}{I_{\text{nom}}}\right)^{\alpha} \cdot |I|
00038|     \label{eq:cycle_aging}
00039| \end{equation}
00040| 
00041| where: $k_{\text{cyc}}(T)$ represents the aging rate constant, which is a function of temperature $T$.$\gamma \approx 2.0$ is the depth of discharge (DOD) exponent$\alpha \approx 0.7$ is the C-rate exponent (a model parameter describing the sensitivity of aging to the C-rate), where the C-rate is a dimensionless ratio defined as current divided by rated capacity.$I_{\text{nom}}$ represents the 1C discharge current.
00042| 
00043| Cycle aging arises from physicochemical fatigue during charge--discharge sequences, with the primary stress factors being the depth of discharge (DOD) and the current rate. Deep cycling induces repeated volume expansion and contraction of electrode materials, leading to mechanical structural damage and cycles of SEI-layer rupture and repair. Meanwhile, high currents exacerbate electrode polarization and can induce harmful side reactions such as lithium plating. Our model describes this process through power-law terms involving DOD and current, quantifying the cumulative damaging effects of fast charging and deep discharging on battery life. Figure~\ref{fig:cycle_aging} demonstrates the cycle aging behavior under various DOD and C-rate conditions.
00044| 
00045| \begin{figure}[htbp]
00046|     \centering
00047|     \includegraphics[width=0.85\textwidth]{fig8_cycle_aging.png}
00048|     \caption{Cycle aging model results. (a) SOH degradation versus cycle number at different depths of discharge (DOD = 20\%, 50\%, 80\%, 100\%) with C-rate = 1C. (b) SOH degradation at different C-rates (0.5C, 1C, 2C, 3C) with DOD = 80\%. Deep cycling and high C-rates significantly accelerate cycle aging.}
00049|     \label{fig:cycle_aging}
00050| \end{figure}
00051| 
00052| \subsection{Combined Aging Model}
00053| \label{subsec:combined_aging}
00054| 
00055| \begin{equation}
00056|     \boxed{\frac{dSOH}{dt} = -\dot{SOH}_{\text{cal}}(T, SOC) - \dot{SOH}_{\text{cyc}}(I, T, SOC)}
00057|     \label{eq:total_aging}
00058| \end{equation}
00059| 
00060| Based on the integrated aging model, it is evident that smartphone battery aging is the result of the combined action of calendar aging and cycle aging.Calendar aging represents continuous degradation occurring even when the device is idle, driven primarily by high temperatures and high states of charge (High SOC). Maintaining a full charge for extended periods or device overheating exponentially accelerates chemical side reactions, resulting in the irreversible consumption of active lithium.Cycle aging occurs during every charge-discharge cycle; deep discharges (high-intensity usage at low battery levels) and high currents from fast charging exacerbate the structural fatigue of electrode materials and the proliferation of the SEI layer.The integrated model indicates that the scenarios most detrimental to battery life are: simultaneous gaming while fast charging (High Temperature + High SOC + High Current) and keeping the device plugged in at full charge for extended periods (High SOC + Potential High Temperature). Consequently, optimizing usage strategies—specifically avoiding prolonged exposure to extreme charge levels (0\% or 100\%), minimizing usage while charging, and controlling device temperature—can significantly mitigate battery aging. Figure~\ref{fig:comprehensive_aging} presents a comprehensive comparison of aging under different usage scenarios.
00061| 
00062| \begin{figure}[htbp]
00063|     \centering
00064|     \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig9_comprehensive_aging.png}
00065|     \caption{Comprehensive aging model analysis. (a) Combined SOH degradation trajectories for different usage scenarios over 3 years: Light Use (20--80\% SOC, 0.5C, 25°C), Normal Use (10--90\% SOC, 1C, 30°C), and Gaming + Fast Charge (0--100\% SOC, 2C, 40°C). (b) Stacked bar chart showing calendar vs.\ cycle aging contributions for each scenario. (c) Heatmap of time to reach 80\% EOL threshold as a function of average C-rate and temperature, demonstrating the strong dependence on both factors.}
00066|     \label{fig:comprehensive_aging}
00067| \end{figure}

(End of file - total 67 lines)
</file>

<file>
00001| \section{Sensitivity Analysis}
00002| \label{sec:sensitivity}
00003| 
00004| \hspace*{\parindent}To identify the key factors influencing smartphone battery life predictions, we conduct a comprehensive sensitivity analysis. The analysis focuses on five critical factors: power consumption, temperature, CPU utilization, screen brightness, and battery health (SOH). We employ both variance-based global sensitivity analysis (Sobol method) and One-at-a-Time (OAT) local sensitivity analysis to provide a complete picture of parameter importance.
00005| 
00006| \subsection{Methodology}
00007| 
00008| \subsubsection{Sobol Variance-Based Global Sensitivity Analysis}
00009| 
00010| The Sobol method decomposes the total variance of the output into contributions from individual input parameters and their interactions. Using Saltelli's sampling scheme with $N=512$ base samples (6,144 model evaluations), we compute:
00011| 
00012| \begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
00013|     \item \textbf{First-order index} $S_1$: The main effect of parameter $X_i$ alone, given by:
00014|     \begin{equation}
00015|         S_{1,i} = \frac{V[E(Y|X_i)]}{V(Y)}
00016|     \end{equation}
00017|     
00018|     \item \textbf{Total-order index} $S_T$: The main effect plus all interactions with other parameters:
00019|     \begin{equation}
00020|         S_{T,i} = 1 - \frac{V[E(Y|X_{\sim i})]}{V(Y)}
00021|     \end{equation}
00022| \end{itemize}
00023| 
00024| \subsubsection{One-at-a-Time (OAT) Analysis}
00025| 
00026| The OAT method varies each parameter independently while holding others at their baseline values. This allows direct visualization of the input-output relationship and calculation of the sensitivity range:
00027| \begin{equation}
00028|     \text{OAT Range} = \frac{t_{\text{empty}}^{\max} - t_{\text{empty}}^{\min}}{t_{\text{empty}}^{\text{mean}}} \times 100\%
00029| \end{equation}
00030| 
00031| \subsection{Analysis Configuration}
00032| 
00033| \begin{table}[H]
00034| \centering
00035| \caption{Parameter ranges for sensitivity analysis}
00036| \label{tab:sa_params}
00037| \begin{tabular}{@{}lccl@{}}
00038| \toprule
00039| \textbf{Factor} & \textbf{Min} & \textbf{Max} & \textbf{Description} \\
00040| \midrule
00041| Power Factor ($P$) & 0.5 & 2.0 & Multiplier on base power consumption \\
00042| Temperature ($T$) & 0$^\circ$C & 45$^\circ$C & Ambient temperature range \\
00043| CPU Utilization ($U_{CPU}$) & 10\% & 100\% & CPU usage fraction \\
00044| Screen Brightness ($B$) & 10\% & 100\% & Display brightness level \\
00045| Battery Health (SOH) & 70\% & 100\% & State of Health (aging level) \\
00046| \bottomrule
00047| \end{tabular}
00048| \end{table}
00049| 
00050| The battery life model integrates these factors through:
00051| \begin{equation}
00052|     t_{\text{empty}} = \frac{Q_{\text{nom}} \cdot \text{SOH} \cdot f_T(T) \cdot (\text{SOC}_0 - \text{SOC}_{\min}) \cdot V_{\text{avg}}}{P_{\text{total}}(U_{CPU}, B, T)}
00053| \end{equation}
00054| 
00055| where $f_T(T)$ is the temperature correction factor and $P_{\text{total}}$ is the total power consumption.
00056| 
00057| \subsection{Results}
00058| 
00059| \subsubsection{Sobol Sensitivity Indices}
00060| 
00061| \begin{table}[H]
00062| \centering
00063| \caption{Sobol sensitivity indices for battery life prediction (ranked by $S_T$)}
00064| \label{tab:sobol_results}
00065| \begin{tabular}{@{}clcccl@{}}
00066| \toprule
00067| \textbf{Rank} & \textbf{Factor} & $\mathbf{S_1}$ & $\mathbf{S_T}$ & \textbf{OAT Range} & \textbf{Class} \\
00068| \midrule
00069| 1 & Power Factor ($P$) & 0.639 & 0.699 & 161.1\% & HIGH \\
00070| 2 & CPU Utilization ($U_{CPU}$) & 0.165 & 0.227 & 70.4\% & MEDIUM \\
00071| 3 & Screen Brightness ($B$) & 0.069 & 0.092 & 45.4\% & LOW \\
00072| 4 & Battery Health (SOH) & 0.042 & 0.054 & 36.9\% & LOW \\
00073| 5 & Temperature ($T$) & 0.012 & 0.016 & 21.5\% & LOW \\
00074| \bottomrule
00075| \end{tabular}
00076| \end{table}
00077| 
00078| \begin{figure}[H]
00079|     \centering
00080|     \includegraphics[width=0.85\textwidth]{fig_sensitivity_sobol.png}
00081|     \caption{Sobol sensitivity indices for the five analyzed factors. Green bars represent first-order indices ($S_1$), while blue bars represent total-order indices ($S_T$). Error bars indicate 95\% confidence intervals.}
00082|     \label{fig:sobol_indices}
00083| \end{figure}
00084| 
00085| \subsubsection{OAT Sensitivity Analysis}
00086| 
00087| \begin{figure}[H]
00088|     \centering
00089|     \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_sensitivity_oat.png}
00090|     \caption{One-at-a-Time sensitivity analysis results. Panels (a)--(e) show the effect of each factor on battery life while holding others at baseline values. Panel (f) summarizes the relative sensitivity ranking.}
00091|     \label{fig:oat_analysis}
00092| \end{figure}
00093| 
00094| \subsubsection{Parameter Interactions}
00095| 
00096| \begin{figure}[H]
00097|     \centering
00098|     \includegraphics[width=0.7\textwidth]{fig_sensitivity_interactions.png}
00099|     \caption{Sensitivity interaction heatmap. Diagonal elements show first-order indices ($S_1$); off-diagonal elements show second-order interaction indices ($S_2$).}
00100|     \label{fig:interactions}
00101| \end{figure}
00102| 
00103| \subsection{Key Findings}
00104| 
00105| Based on the comprehensive sensitivity analysis, we identify the following key findings:
00106| 
00107| \begin{enumerate}
00108|     \item \textbf{Power consumption dominates battery life} ($S_T = 0.699$): 
00109|     The power factor accounts for nearly 70\% of the total output variance. This result is expected since battery life is inversely proportional to power consumption. The OAT analysis shows a 161.1\% range variation, confirming that power management is the primary lever for extending battery life.
00110|     
00111|     \item \textbf{CPU utilization is the most important controllable factor} ($S_T = 0.227$):
00112|     Among factors that can be directly controlled by users and OS designers, CPU utilization has the highest sensitivity. Reducing CPU load from 100\% to 30\% can extend battery life by approximately 50\%. This highlights the importance of efficient background process management.
00113|     
00114|     \item \textbf{Screen brightness has moderate impact} ($S_T = 0.092$):
00115|     Display brightness contributes about 9\% to the output variance. The OAT analysis shows that dimming from 100\% to 50\% extends battery life by approximately 20\%. Adaptive brightness algorithms can provide significant power savings.
00116|     
00117|     \item \textbf{Battery health (SOH) has limited short-term effect} ($S_T = 0.054$):
00118|     While SOH directly affects available capacity, its sensitivity is relatively low because the typical operating range (70\%--100\%) corresponds to only a 30\% capacity variation. However, SOH becomes increasingly important for aged batteries approaching end-of-life.
00119|     
00120|     \item \textbf{Temperature effects are surprisingly negligible} ($S_T = 0.016$):
00121|     Within the normal operating range (0--45$^\circ$C), temperature has minimal direct impact on battery life. This is because the temperature correction factor $f_T(T)$ only significantly reduces capacity at extreme cold temperatures ($<$10$^\circ$C).
00122| \end{enumerate}
00123| 
00124| \subsection{Comprehensive Sensitivity Summary}
00125| 
00126| \begin{figure}[H]
00127|     \centering
00128|     \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_sensitivity_comprehensive.png}
00129|     \caption{Comprehensive sensitivity analysis summary: (a) Sobol indices comparison, (b) Tornado diagram showing impact ranges, (c) normalized OAT response curves, and (d) classification summary table.}
00130|     \label{fig:comprehensive}
00131| \end{figure}
00132| 
00133| \subsection{Practical Recommendations}
00134| 
00135| Based on the sensitivity analysis results, we provide the following recommendations:
00136| 
00137| \subsubsection{For Users}
00138| 
00139| \begin{enumerate}[noitemsep]
00140|     \item \textbf{Reduce background CPU processes} (Highest impact): 
00141|     Close unnecessary background applications and disable auto-refresh features. A 30\% reduction in CPU utilization yields approximately 42\% improvement in battery life.
00142|     
00143|     \item \textbf{Use adaptive screen brightness}:
00144|     Enable automatic brightness adjustment or manually reduce brightness in dim environments. Reducing brightness from 100\% to 50\% extends battery life by approximately 18\%.
00145|     
00146|     \item \textbf{Maintain healthy charging habits}:
00147|     Keep the battery between 20\%--80\% SOC to slow aging. Avoid exposing the device to extreme temperatures while charging.
00148| \end{enumerate}
00149| 
00150| \subsubsection{For OS Designers}
00151| 
00152| \begin{enumerate}[noitemsep]
00153|     \item \textbf{Prioritize CPU power efficiency}:
00154|     Implement intelligent CPU frequency scaling and process scheduling. The high sensitivity of CPU utilization ($S_T = 0.227$) justifies investment in advanced power management algorithms.
00155|     
00156|     \item \textbf{Implement context-aware brightness control}:
00157|     Use ambient light sensors and usage patterns to optimize display brightness dynamically.
00158|     
00159|     \item \textbf{Focus testing on usage patterns}:
00160|     The low temperature sensitivity ($S_T = 0.016$) suggests that environmental testing can be simplified, while more resources should be allocated to testing diverse usage scenarios.
00161| \end{enumerate}
00162| 
00163| \subsection{Model Robustness}
00164| 
00165| The sensitivity analysis also provides insights into model robustness:
00166| 
00167| \begin{itemize}[noitemsep]
00168|     \item \textbf{Sum of first-order indices}: $\sum S_1 = 0.927$, indicating that first-order effects explain 92.7\% of the variance.
00169|     \item \textbf{Interaction effects}: The gap between $\sum S_T$ and $\sum S_1$ is small, suggesting limited parameter interactions.
00170|     \item \textbf{Prediction stability}: $\pm$10\% variation in key parameters results in only $\pm$15\% change in predicted battery life, confirming model stability.
00171| \end{itemize}
00172|

<file>
00001| \section{Introduction}
00002| \label{sec:introduction}
00003| 
00004| \subsection{Research Background}
00005| \label{subsec:research-background}
00006| 
00007| \hspace*{\parindent}The ubiquity of smartphones has fundamentally transformed human communication and working paradigms. Statistics project that the global smartphone user base will exceed 7 billion by 2026, with average daily usage surpassing 4.5 hours. This high-frequency usage scenario places a substantial strain on battery systems, rendering battery performance a critical metric influencing user satisfaction and device utility.
00008| 
00009| As the predominant power source, Lithium-ion batteries operate via complex mechanisms that are highly susceptible to variables such as temperature, discharge rates, usage patterns, and aging. Consequently, it is of paramount importance for general users to understand how to optimize battery endurance and prolong lifespan through appropriate usage habits.
00010| 
00011| \vspace{0.5\baselineskip}
00012| \begin{center}
00013|     \includegraphics[width=0.8\textwidth]{figures/image.jpeg}
00014| \end{center}
00015| 
00016| \subsection{Problem Restatement}
00017| \label{subsec:problem}
00018| 
00019| \hspace*{\parindent}Based on the background discussed above, our goal is to develop a continuous-time mathematical model for a smartphone lithium-ion battery that predicts the State of Charge (SOC) as a function of time and the time-to-empty under various usage conditions. The model should address the following tasks:
00020| 
00021| \begin{itemize}
00022|     \item \textbf{Develop a Continuous-Time SOC Model:} Grounded in fundamental battery mechanisms, construct a dynamic SOC equation that incorporates multi-factor influences---including screen usage, processor load, and network activity---and perform parameter estimation and validation using publicly available data.
00023| 
00024|     \item \textbf{Predict Time-to-Empty and Analyze Drivers:} Compute the time-to-empty across representative scenarios, compare predictions against observed behaviors, and identify the key drivers responsible for rapid battery drain.
00025| 
00026|     \item \textbf{Examine Model Sensitivity:} Analyze how prediction outcomes change with modeling assumptions, parameter values, and fluctuations in usage patterns.
00027| 
00028|     \item \textbf{Provide Recommendations and Generalization:} Formulate practical guidelines for extending battery life based on model insights, and discuss potential applications in power-saving strategy design and generalization to other portable devices.
00029| \end{itemize}
00030| 
00031| \subsection{Our Work}
00032| \label{subsec:our-work}
00033| 
00034| \hspace*{\parindent}We adopt a \textbf{task-driven, multi-model framework} that systematically addresses each problem requirement. The overall workflow of our approach is illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:workflow}.
00035| 
00036| \begin{figure}[H]
00037|     \centering
00038|     \begin{tikzpicture}[
00039|         node distance=0.6cm and 0.8cm,
00040|         startstop/.style={rectangle, rounded corners, minimum width=2.2cm, minimum height=0.6cm, text centered, draw=black, fill=green!20, font=\footnotesize},
00041|         process/.style={rectangle, minimum width=2.4cm, minimum height=0.6cm, text centered, draw=black, fill=blue!15, font=\footnotesize},

00043|         output/.style={rectangle, minimum width=2.2cm, minimum height=0.6cm, text centered, draw=black, fill=red!15, font=\footnotesize},
00044|         arrow/.style={thick,->,>=stealth}
00045|     ]
00046|     
00047|     % Row 1: Problem Analysis
00048|     \node (start) [startstop] {Problem Analysis};
00049|     \node (task1) [process, right=of start] {Task 1: SOC Model};
00050|     \node (task2) [process, right=of task1] {Task 2: Prediction};

00064|

<file>
00001| \section{Strengths and Weaknesses}
00002| \label{sec:posneg}
00003| 
00004| \subsection{Strengths}
00005| 
00006| \begin{enumerate}
00007|     \item \textbf{Continuous-Time ODE Formulation.} 
00008|     The five-state ODE system provides natural representation of battery dynamics, flexibility in time-step selection, and seamless integration with control systems.
00009|     
00010|     \item \textbf{Multi-Physics Coupling.} 
00011|     Our model integrates electrochemical (SOC, voltage), thermal, and degradation processes within a unified framework, capturing critical interactions often neglected in simpler models.
00012|     
00013|     \item \textbf{Computational Efficiency.} 
00014|     The 2-RC equivalent circuit model achieves real-time capability ($<$1~ms per step) with only 6--8 parameters, compared to 50+ parameters for full electrochemical models.
00015|     
00016|     \item \textbf{Uncertainty Quantification.} 
00017|     Monte Carlo propagation provides confidence intervals for all predictions, enabling risk-aware battery management decisions.
00018|     
00019|     \item \textbf{Actionable Sensitivity Analysis.} 
00020|     Sobol-based global sensitivity analysis identifies prioritized user actions (initial SOC: $S_T=0.71$, CPU utilization: $S_T=0.39$), enabling targeted optimization.
00021|     
00022|     \item \textbf{Validated Accuracy.} 
00023|     Drive cycle validation demonstrates SOC RMSE of 2.1\%, terminal voltage RMSE of 18~mV, and time-to-empty prediction error below 5\%.
00024| \end{enumerate}
00025| 
00026| \subsection{Weaknesses and Mitigations}
00027| 
00028| \begin{enumerate}
00029|     \item \textbf{Lumped Thermal Model.} 
00030|     Single-temperature assumption may underestimate internal gradients in large cells or during fast charging.
00031|     \textit{Mitigation:} Extend to 1D spatial discretization for high-power scenarios.
00032|     
00033|     \item \textbf{Semi-Empirical Aging.} 
00034|     The Arrhenius-based aging model captures calendar and cycle aging but lacks electrochemical mechanistic detail for SOH $<$70\%.
00035|     \textit{Mitigation:} Incorporate physics-based SEI growth models for long-term predictions.
00036|     
00037|     \item \textbf{Usage Pattern Dependency.} 
00038|     Predictions assume stable usage within the forecast horizon; sudden mode changes degrade accuracy.
00039|     \textit{Mitigation:} Integrate usage prediction models with rolling forecast updates.
00040|     
00041|     \item \textbf{Limited Extreme Condition Accuracy.} 
00042|     Model validated for 0--40$^\circ$C; accuracy degrades at sub-zero temperatures.
00043|     \textit{Mitigation:} Add low-temperature lithium plating sub-model.
00044|     
00045|     \item \textbf{Component Power Simplification.} 
00046|     OLED content-dependent power and background process variations are simplified.
00047|     \textit{Mitigation:} Device-specific power profiling with runtime learning.
00048| \end{enumerate}
00049| 
00050| \subsection{Comparison with Alternative Approaches}
00051| 
00052| \begin{table}[H]
00053|     \centering
00054|     \caption{Comparison of battery modeling approaches for smartphone applications.}
00055|     \label{tab:model_comparison}
00056|     \begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
00057|         \toprule
00058|         \textbf{Aspect} & \textbf{Our Model} & \textbf{Data-Driven} & \textbf{Electrochemical} \\
00059|         \midrule
00060|         Prediction Accuracy & Good (2--5\%) & Variable & Excellent ($<$1\%) \\
00061|         Computational Cost & Low ($<$1~ms) & Medium & High (minutes) \\
00062|         Real-Time Capable & Yes & Yes & No \\
00063|         Interpretability & High & Low & High \\
00064|         Calibration Effort & Medium & Low & High \\
00065|         Generalizability & Good & Limited & Excellent \\
00066|         \bottomrule
00067|     \end{tabular}
00068| \end{table}
00069| 
00070| Our ODE-based approach balances accuracy, efficiency, and interpretability, making it well-suited for real-time smartphone battery management where computational resources are constrained and actionable insights are valued.
00071| 

(End of file - total 71 lines)
</file>

00001| \section{Conclusions and Recommendations}
00002| \label{sec:conclusion}
00003| 
00004| \subsection{Summary of Contributions}
00005| \label{subsec:summary}
00006| 
00007| This paper developed a comprehensive continuous-time mathematical model for smartphone battery State of Charge, addressing all aspects of the MCM Problem A. Our key contributions include:
00008| 
00009| \begin{enumerate}
00010|     \item \textbf{Five-State ODE Model:} A coupled electrochemical-thermal-aging system that captures SOC dynamics with 2.1\% RMSE accuracy.
00011|     
00012|     \item \textbf{Time-to-Empty Prediction:} An energy-based framework with Monte Carlo uncertainty quantification, providing predictions with 95\% confidence intervals.
00013|     
00014|     \item \textbf{Global Sensitivity Analysis:} Rigorous identification of key factors (brightness, CPU load) and surprisingly minor factors (GPS, internal resistance).
00015|     
00016|     \item \textbf{Actionable Recommendations:} Quantified improvement potentials for user behaviors and OS optimization strategies.
00017| \end{enumerate}
00018|

00031|

00033| 
00034| \begin{enumerate}
00035|     \item \textbf{Machine Learning Enhancement:} Use neural networks to learn residual errors and improve predictions.
00036|     
00037|     \item \textbf{Distributed Thermal Model:} Implement 1D or 2D thermal models for better temperature prediction during high-power events.
00038|     
00039|     \item \textbf{Usage Pattern Learning:} Develop personalized models that learn individual user behavior patterns.
00040|     
00041|     \item \textbf{Cloud-Based Calibration:} Leverage fleet data to continuously improve parameter estimates.
00042|     
00043|     \item \textbf{Multi-Cell Extensions:} Extend the model to devices with multiple battery cells.
00044| \end{enumerate}

  "newString": "\\subsection{Power-to-Current Conversion}\n\\label{subsec:power_current}\n\n\\hspace*{\\parindent}In practical use, smartphones operate in different working states, each associated with a distinct power consumption profile, which leads to different SOC trajectories. In this model, we assume there is no direct coupling among the power draws of different hardware modules; hence, the total power consumption is approximated as the sum of the individual component powers.\n\n\\begin{equation}\n    P_{\\text{total}}(t) = P_{\\text{display}} + P_{\\text{CPU}} + P_{\\text{network}} + P_{\\text{GPS}} + P_{\\text{base}} + P_{\\text{other}}\n    \\label{eq:power_total}\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n    \\centering\n    \\includegraphics[width=0.7\\textwidth]{figures/B05BE26D1ADE5254D2A58C0F9CCE878C.png}\n    \\caption{Power consumption component models and parameters for smartphone hardware. Each component follows a specific power model based on utilization, with display and CPU being the dominant contributors.}\n    \\label{fig:power_model_table}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n    \\centering\n    \\includegraphics[width=0.55\\textwidth]{figures/DE80970C2134029C2340572AAFAE6797.png}\n    \\caption{Power consumption breakdown under typical usage scenario (total 2.10~W). The CPU accounts for the largest share (40.5\\%), followed by GPS (23.8\\%) and display (21.4\\%).}\n    \\label{fig:power_breakdown}\n\\end{figure}",
  "oldString": "\\subsection{Power-to-Current Conversion}\n\\label{subsec:power_current}\n\n\\hspace*{\\parindent}In practical use, smartphones operate in different working states, each associated with a distinct power consumption profile, which leads to different SOC trajectories. Therefore, we introduce a power-consumption model to represent parameter differences across operating states. In this model, we assume there is no direct coupling among the power draws of different hardware modules; hence, the total power consumption is approximated as the sum of the individual component powers.\n\n\\begin{equation}\n    P_{\\text{total}}(t) = P_{\\text{display}} + P_{\\text{CPU}} + P_{\\text{network}} + P_{\\text{GPS}} + P_{\\text{base}} + P_{\\text{other}}\n    \\label{eq:power_total}\n\\end{equation}"
}
